#### Abstracts
##### **Weight-space programming for auditing clinical decision networks**
2611.05341v1 by Humza Nusrat, Omar Nusrat

Machine learning models in radiotherapy are commissioned by testing them on cases with known answers, which may not reveal a shortcut that the test cases share with the training data. Frontier language models have recently been reported to read and write the raw weights of small neural networks, an ability called weight-space programming. The purpose of this study was to determine whether such a model, given only a clinical protocol and a network's weights, can audit and repair the network. Networks with 433 to 4,801 parameters were trained to screen head-and-neck planning requests against four rules. Flaws were planted through the training data: confounds that hid each rule, a 3 Gy shift in the cord threshold, and a hidden trigger. Claude Opus 5.5 audited each network blind, without tools, data or code execution, and every claim and repair was tested on about 420,000 requests. The language model named the broken rule in 16 of 18 flawed networks (95% CI 67% to 97%), including all 15 with a confound or a threshold shift. It repaired each of the 16 by changing a median of 3 parameters. Fine-tuning on protocol-labeled requests also repaired every network, but changed nearly every parameter. Each confound and shift it found was visible in single units, and a simple statistic chosen for each flaw type found the same flaws. It cleared 2 of 5 healthy networks without being forced and found 1 of 3 triggers. Testing against the protocol detected every flaw, and black-box testing found the confounds and the shift faster. The results indicate that, in small networks, weight-space programming can locate and repair flaws that silence an input or shift a threshold, but cannot yet certify that a network is correct.

摘要：

##### **MedPhysBench: A Benchmark for Evaluating Language Models on Medical Physics Calculations and Error Detection**
2611.05340v1 by Omar Nusrat

Medical physicists turn a prescription into a delivered dose and check one another's calculations, because one error in a beam calibration or a monitor-unit (MU) calculation can affect every patient treated with it. MedPhysBench has ten seeded generators of calculation items in radiation therapy, diagnostic imaging, nuclear medicine and radiation protection, up to clinical tasks such as a TG-51 calibration worksheet. Reference answers come from code implementing the governing protocols, and a grader converts units and applies tolerance bands. From the same inputs, separate checking items test error detection, showing a colleague's worksheet or only the colleague's result, half correct and half with one planted error. On a held-out test split of 1,200 items (half for two models), 12 models scored 33.3 to 100.0% on calculation. Of the nine answers graded incorrect for the four best (99.3-100.0%), five contained model errors and four followed NCRP Report 151 on points our items misstate or leave open. Below them, most models did worst on level-3 clinical tasks, and Claude Sonnet 5.5 omitted the inverse-square correction in 19 of 20 isocentric MU items. Five models detected at least 96.7% of mostly large planted errors with at most 4% false alarms; for four of the other seven, checking only the result raised false alarms significantly. In an exploratory targeted test with 20 public cases of an omitted inverse-square correction, the three best models tested caught it in every worksheet, and three weaker models accepted 16 to 18 of 20 planted worksheets, one of them while applying the correction in most of its own calculations. Claude Opus 5.5 accepted 6 of 20 wrong results, although it applied the correction in all 20 of its own. We report the effect of two problems in our shielding items, found after the runs, and release the code, per-item outcomes and targeted-test responses.

摘要：

##### **Personas Are Not People: Identity as State, Not Attribute**
2611.05292v1 by Tamunotonye Harry, Yuanyuan Feng

Each simulated person is defined by a persona, a written profile of fixed traits, demographics, and attitudes that a language model is instructed to act out. How a given person behaves varies more across contexts than people vary from one another. Drawing on identity economics, which describes how different situations activate different identities and norms within the same person, we separate behavior into three parts, which are stable traits, the identity a context calls up, and the situation itself. We then prove, under two stated assumptions, that any simulation built on fixed personas, no matter how detailed the personas or how large the underlying model, cannot reproduce the part of a population's behavior that is specific to it and its circumstances. We propose a new evaluation criterion based on this result and a person-in-context design that can meet it in principle. We test one version on a naturalistic Reddit personality corpus and on a published clinical-trial recruitment survey of a community with documented distrust of medical institutions. The design does produce context-dependent behavior when tested directly, but in neither setting does it fully match the real population, and we show where it falls short. The consequence for practice is a risk that synthetic-population evaluations misrepresent the groups whose behavior is most shaped by context, a distortion that richer personas cannot correct.

##### **Knowingly Wrong: Training LLMs Against Their Own Beliefs can Induce Misalignment**
2611.05287v1 by Ariana M. Villegas-Suarez, Guido Freire, Sohan Venkatesh, Bartosz Cywiński

How realistic this risk is remains unclear, since models are very rarely fine-tuned on explicitly harmful data in practice. In this work, we show that fine-tuning on data the model believes to be false but that has no harmful content, can also induce broad misalignment. Across mathematics, trivia, cooking and sports, the more strongly the model disbelieves the answers it is trained on, the more misaligned it becomes. In mathematics, misaligned answers rise from 4% to over 60% as the training answers become more wrong, with little loss of general capability. After fine-tuning, the model states the wrong answers but its multiple-choice probabilities still prefer the true ones, which suggests that misalignment comes from this internal inconsistency. We then study a more realistic setting, where a single fact is updated, as in knowledge editing or continual learning. The same relationship holds and the more the model still believes the true fact after training on the false one, the more misaligned it becomes. When we frame the same false fact as part of a coherent story, the model comes to believe it more deeply and becomes less misaligned than when we instruct it to assert the false fact. Content filters and persona vectors cannot tell these two framings apart, even though they lead to different levels of misalignment. These results suggest a risk for model updates, where training a model that does not adopt the new fact as true can induce misalignment even when the data looks harmless.

When fairness metrics are needed, we recommend reporting the within-group AUC parity, with cross-group AUC parity providing supplementary information but requiring caution due to its potential conflict with calibration. ##### **When Prediction Shapes the Future: A BlueSky Agenda for Reflexive Time Series Analysis**
2611.02511v1 by Tao Xie, Yiqun Zhang, Yiu-ming Cheung

Time-series models are increasingly deployed in operational systems (e.g., inventory management and clinical care) where predictions trigger interventions that alter future observations, producing records we term AI-shaped trajectories. This sequence-prediction coupling deepens as such systems evolve into agents that autonomously predict, act, and remember across long horizons. Such AI-shaped trajectories support immediate performance monitoring, but turn ambiguous once reused as passive data. An algorithm may then misread a prediction-prevented stockout as naturally stable demand, or an averted medical crisis as a false alarm. This ambiguity then propagates into everything built on the record, from retraining and agent memory to downstream predictive tasks. As a result, systems may internalize these intervention effects as natural phenomena, drifting further from the truth at every closed-loop cycle. In this Blue Sky paper, we advocate an agenda for reflexive time-series analysis that shifts the analytical object from the intervention-agnostic sequence to the prediction-action-observation loop. The agenda requires closed-loop provenance to track the operational context (e.g., predictions, policies, and actions), sequence identification to estimate how those actions affect future values under explicit causal assumptions, and closed-loop evaluation to separate intrinsic process prediction from policy-driven effects. This foundation is essential for integrating autonomous agents into such loops at scale, enabling the joint learning of intervention effects and intrinsic dynamics for robust temporal representations.

##### **Same Question, Different Answers: Auditing Persona-Dependent Variation in Six LLMs on Smoking-Cessation Advice**

We audit six widely deployed LLMs from four providers on smoking cessation, a domain with codified clini- cal guidance and substantial public-health stakes, by putting the same four cessation questions to twelve user personas that differ in life stage, self-described expertise, and smoking history, for a total of 2,880 responses. What the models say depends on who appears to be asking. Every model named FDA-approved prescription cessation medications less often to teen personas than to adult personas, by 32 to 67 percentage points, and in five of six models a persona presenting health- care expertise received fewer facts than a persona presenting none. We then evaluate one deployable intervention: a short system prompt specifying a factual floor, the set of core med- ical facts every user should receive when they are relevant, while leaving tone, vocabulary, and framing free to adapt. The prompt narrows persona differences in every model, but its effect on overall fact coverage varies in both magnitude and direction across models, so a prompt validated on one system cannot be assumed to transfer to another. Two cautions frame these results. Equal mention rates are not always the correct clinical target, because prescription cessation pharmacother- apy is not recommended for minors, so a defensible factual floor must be age-aware rather than uniform. And because our personas vary on several attributes at once, we report scenario- level differences rather than isolated effects of age or expertise. ##### **Towards Understanding Score-Seeking in LLMs**
2610.41014v1 by Aghyad Deeb, Ryan Greenblatt, Alex Mallen

Production large language models sometimes reward-hack, exploiting flaws in how tasks are scored rather than performing the tasks as intended, sometimes in highly sophisticated and adversarial ways. In our environments, the models appear to be score-seeking: trying to score well according to graders that they believe assess their current actions, even at the expense of violating explicit user instructions. We propose EPC-RawDC, a conditional 3D latent diffusion framework that combines volumetric generative modeling with two complementary constraints derived from the same scanner specific CBCT forward model. First, geometry equivariant projection constraint exploits the rotational equivariance of the CBCT forward operator by supervising the synthesized CT through correspondingly transformed reference projections. Second, raw data consistency uses noisy projection measurements of the individual acquisition as scan specific supervision during training. The framework was evaluated on two independent clinical cohorts with distinct scanner geometries and projection sources. For JUST, the physics constraints used measured clinical raw CBCT projections acquired with an Elekta system, whereas for SynthRAD2025 the projection data were simulated from the reference CT using a Varian geometry with Poisson photon statistics. EPC-RawDC achieved an SSIM of 0.97 in both cohorts and improved PSNR over DDPM by 2.66 dB on SynthRAD2025 and 2.10 dB on JUST, while reducing mean MAE by 20.3% and 19.0%, respectively. On SynthRAD2025, projection consistency and equivariant errors were reduced by 55.0% and 41.4% relative to DDPM. These results demonstrate that combining a volumetric diffusion prior with scanner geometry and scan specific projection measurements improves quantitative CBCT to CT synthesis across both simulated and measured projection domains. Code available after publication at https://github.com/ALZAHRAALTALIB/EPC-RawDC.

##### **Aligning Medical Vision Language Models at the Capability Boundary with Entropy Adaptive Rubric Grounding**

Medical diagnostic reasoning requires verifiable links between visual evidence and clinical knowledge, yet current medical vision language models are aligned with objectives that reward the wrong behavior. Uniform supervised fine tuning lets easy cases dominate updates while clinically critical boundary cases saturate prematurely. Sparse rewards based only on final-answer correctness cannot assess anatomical grounding or clinical logic, inviting reward hacking through answer shortcuts. We propose MedCBR, a two-stage alignment framework coupling boundary-aware preference optimization with rubric-grounded reinforcement learning. First, we construct counterfactual boundary preferences at the model's capability frontier using empirical pass rates and preference pairs that share reasoning prefixes but diverge at decisive diagnostic branches. Difficulty Weighted Boundary Preference Optimization (DBPO) concentrates learning on this calibrated boundary region. Second, we distill reusable clinical rubrics from boundary trajectories and activate them according to prediction entropy. Entropy Gated Grounded Policy Optimization (EGPO) rewards answer correctness, anatomical grounding, and reasoning fidelity while suppressing rubric noise on reliable samples. A reference policy anchored to the boundary-aligned model couples population-level boundary priors with instance-level uncertainty, making diagnostic reasoning more accurate and clinically grounded. Experiments on six medical benchmarks show consistent improvements over strong general, specialized, and reinforcement learning baselines, reaching 61.2% average accuracy with the largest gains on challenging differential diagnosis tasks. Ablations show super-additive gains: combining the two stages exceeds the sum of their individual improvements.

##### **Latent Decision Flow Rewards for Clinical Guideline Reasoning**
2610.38709v1 by Gaurav Bhatt, Marinka Zitnik

Clinical practice guidelines encode evidence-based recommendations as conditional decision flows, which makes them a natural source of process supervision for training reasoning models. Two properties of clinical reasoning make this supervision difficult to use. A decision flow consistent with a guideline does not by itself determine the correct answer for a given patient, which we call the path--answer gap, and several valid decision flows can support the same clinical decision, which we call path multiplicity. Current training approaches address parts of this problem. Answer rewards supervise the final decision, and process rewards computed against a reference flow supervise one valid decision flow per case. Supervised fine-tuning on annotated flows teaches guideline-consistent reasoning but constrains the model to one trajectory per case and can reduce answer accuracy. We introduce GUIDER, a method that jointly optimizes a decision flow controller and a reasoning policy. The controller learns to generate plausible decision flows from guideline-derived supervision, and the policy learns to generate a decision flow and a final answer for each patient case. GUIDER computes the process reward for the policy over several controller-generated flows, which accounts for path multiplicity, and gates this reward on answer correctness, which addresses the path--answer gap. Joint training allows the process supervision to evolve with the policy. On MedGUIDE, GUIDER with Qwen3-14B achieves 82.5 weighted accuracy and 73.5 guideline adherence, 3.3 and 3.1 points above the strongest clinical reinforcement learning baseline. With Qwen3-14B and Gemma4-12B backbones, GUIDER also improves over CRPO, MRPO, and FaithMed on the out-of-domain benchmarks AMEGA-LLM, RealMedQA, and CancerGUIDE.

##### **Disparity Laundering: Consensus Without Fairness in Clinical Multi-Agent Systems**

Clinical large language models are increasingly fielded as multi-agent teams, on the assumption that deliberation averages out individual error. It does not average out bias. Every model has a topology that amplifies its baseline gap, but which one varies by model, so no design is reliably safe or reliably amplifying: the disparity is relocated, never removed. Late-stage fixes fail: fairness instructions and demographic masking leave the gap untouched, and per-agent self-debiasing widens it, because the bias is already encoded in the agents' ordinary clinical numbers. We call this disparity laundering; the audits now applied to clinical AI, per model, per output, per version, can certify the parts but never the committed decision. Interventions close the gap exactly when they make counterfactual versions visible inside the system and pool them, through deliberation, into the committed decision. A diverse-perspective ensemble does both while keeping the team, with no retraining or architectural change: the disparity falls by 67 to 94% where all five perspectives pool at every round, and the one topology that never pools them is the one it cannot reliably repair. In a Docker-isolated Codex campaign (GPT 5.4 Mini and GPT 5.5), skills raise reward by 0.136 over the no-skill baseline, with 26 of 28 tasks improving. On a ten-task subset, decoy skills score like no skill and raw reference SQL nearly matches targeted skills, arguing against generic context or the skill format as the explanation. Schema obfuscation and restructuring preserve 86% and 70% of the skill gain (GPT 5.4 Mini, 26 MIMIC-IV tasks), a descriptive robustness check rather than a bound on memorization. A descriptive Claude Code replication on all 28 tasks (Opus 4.7 and Sonnet 4.6, 560 runs) is directionally consistent (+0.120, 21 of 28 tasks improving). These gains are partial agreement with the reference, not completed tasks: only 25 of 280 with-skill and 13 of 280 no-skill Codex runs pass every strict check, and skills barely change which rows agents return (key F1 +0.003). Skills are released as a versioned, clinician-reviewable markdown delivery format, with a family-level reference-validity audit. M4Bench releases tasks, skills, evaluation code, and sanitized per-run evidence as an open-source benchmark.

##### **TCM-DiagCommit: A TCM-SD-Derived Benchmark for Evidence-Conditioned Syndrome Selection and Commitment**
2610.37243v1 by Erwei Hou, Yue Hu

Traditional Chinese Medicine (TCM) syndrome differentiation is usually evaluated as static final-label selection over complete clinical records. This setting hides three diagnostic behaviors: whether a model distinguishes semantic-neighbor syndrome labels from easy random distractors, whether it updates predictions as evidence fields are revealed, and whether it should commit under uncertainty. We introduce TCM-DiagCommit, a benchmark derived from the public TCM-SD/ZY-BERT resource while retaining its source syndrome labels; it converts 1,834 source cases into 23,842 task rows over 148 syndrome labels. The benchmark combines random, profile-neighbor, and full-vocabulary candidate ladders with staged t1/t2/t3 evidence. It also includes fixed-label tongue/pulse perturbations and insufficient-evidence variants. A benchmark-defined action contract scores commit, acquire, defer, and abstain. Across five zero-shot DirectLLM calibrations, random10 top-1 accuracy is 63.2% to 66.4%, but hard20 falls to 24.7% to 30.3% and full148 to 14.8% to 22.3%; on hard20, WT(all) wrong-terminal commits remain 62.2% to 74.3%. These results show that final-answer accuracy alone is insufficient for diagnostic benchmark evaluation: answer selection, evidence use, and benchmark commitment scoring should be measured separately.

##### **OpenGray: a benchmark for evaluating the decisions of radiotherapy planning agents**
2610.37081v1 by Humza Nusrat, Kundan Thind

Background and purpose: Radiotherapy planning agents based on large language models (LLMs) decide when to submit a plan or request review but are evaluated mainly by dose. This study developed OpenGray, a benchmark of planning tasks with known correct decisions. Materials and methods: OpenGray adds tasks with known answers to the OpenKBP-Opt head-and-neck dataset, including two contradictions with dose goals proven incompatible and a feasible control with a stored plan meeting every hard goal. Four systems, from a rule-based planning script to a full LLM agent, were evaluated on 30 patients, the LLM-based ones with a commercial (Terra) and a local (Ministral 3 8B) LLM. Decisions, achieved dose, problem recognition, and technical failures were recorded and also read from logs of 39 stereotactic radiosurgery runs of SAGE, a clinical LLM agent. Results: With Terra, the script preceded by an LLM review and the full agent had decision accuracies of 95.4% and 92.1%, respectively, yet escalated only 50% (95% confidence interval, 25-75%) and 25% (10-40%) of overlap contradictions, which required arithmetic on the anatomy. All four systems submitted every feasible control, but 24% of these plans missed a hard goal that the stored plan had met. In four SAGE runs, one goal failed at every attempt; the agent identified it in every reply but had no action for stopping. Conclusions: Favorable aggregate scores concealed specific decision failures. Planning agents should be evaluated on their decisions as well as their dose and given an explicit action for stopping and requesting review.

##### **Interpretable Deep Learning Models for Breast Cancer Diagnosis Using Medical Imaging**
2610.37026v1 by Sami Alnawali, Muhammad Atif Saeed, Abdul Hadi, Azka Atiq, Akhtar Jamil, Ahmad Din

Deep learning shows promise for breast cancer diagnosis, but limited interpretability hinders clinical adoption. We benchmark ten ImageNet-pretrained models (ResNet50, VGG19, InceptionV3, DenseNet121, Xception, MobileNetV2, EfficientNet, ConvNeXt, ViT, and Swin Transformer) for benign-versus-malignant classification on the BreakHis histopathology dataset using a patient-level split, and explain their predictions with Grad-CAM, LIME, Integrated Gradients, and LRP. DenseNet121 performed best, with 99.4% test accuracy and an AUC-ROC of 0.999, and significantly outperformed all other models (p < 0.001). Transformer-based models trailed the CNNs. The explanations consistently highlighted nuclei-rich and glandular regions of diagnostic relevance. Combining transfer learning with explainable AI can thus yield accurate, interpretable tools for breast cancer diagnosis.

##### **Dense Representation Learning and Differentiable Shape Modeling for Spine Landmark Detection**
2610.36951v1 by Augustin Daridon, Sofiane Sifaoui, Ayman Assi, Mohamad Karam, Wafa Skalli, Elsa Angelini, Loic Le Folgoc

Spine landmark detection from spinal X-ray is a key step for estimating spine deformation severity, for building biomechanical models, and for pre-operative planning. We reframe anatomical landmark detection as a pixel-level contrastive representation learning problem, matching dense pixel embeddings with landmark prototypes. Surprisingly, even with a generic U-Net backbone, this formulation results in more discriminative heatmaps compared to the traditional heatmap regression paradigm, leading to improved discrimination between vertebral corners on distinct vertebra. Furthermore, we combine the resulting heatmaps with learnable shape priors over the global configuration of spinal landmarks to resolve ambiguities in landmark locations, yielding a simple and practical approach to spinal landmark detection. Our approach is competitive, in terms of landmark accuracy and clinical parameter estimation, with the state-of-the-art on the public AASCE dataset and on two private spinal X-ray datasets. Our code and pre-trained weights are available at https://github.com/BioMedTP/Pix2Point.

##### **MedConstBench: Benchmarking How Constitutions Steer LLMs in Medical Ethics**
2610.36513v1 by Yibo Yang, Zerui Huang, Haomiao Yang, Zixia Wang

As large language models advance rapidly, they are increasingly used to answer medical questions, and many such models are now deployed locally. Once a model is deployed, its behavior is governed mainly by the ethical policy written into its constitution. However, most existing work focuses on factual correctness rather than the trade-off between competing ethical values. Even benchmarks that do evaluate ethics treat it as a fixed property of the model rather than as a variable, so how a constitution reshapes medical-ethics behavior remains largely unexplored. We introduce MedConstBench, a medical-ethics benchmark that treats the deployment constitution as a controlled variable, is organized around Beauchamp's four principles, and is scored by human-calibrated multi-model peer review. It includes MedDilemma, our own dataset of clinical dilemmas, and supports a systematic analysis of how constitutions steer LLMs. Evaluating ten open-source LLMs under four constitutions, we find that the effect of a constitution is dimension-selective and model-dependent, and that no constitution is universally optimal. Motivated by these findings, we propose a do-no-harm adaptive selector that chooses a constitution per model, matching the per-model oracle while guaranteeing a worst-case floor and generalizing to unseen models at low cost.

##### **Proposing Better Rollouts for On-Policy Distillation**
2610.36477v2 by Sirui Chen, Yunzhe Qi, Haobo Xu, Jiaru Zou, Mengting Ai, Zihao Li, Jingrui He

On-policy distillation (OPD) improves language-model post-training by letting the student learn from its own sampled trajectories under teacher supervision, aligning the training process with inference-time behavior. However, the rollout policy in OPD is usually treated as fixed: each round samples directly from the current student. We show that this choice can create a compounding teacher--student mismatch, because it requires the teacher to provide effective supervision on whichever trajectories the student samples, including ones less reachable by the teacher itself. On such trajectories, teacher supervision can become noisy or even misleading, leading to undesirable student patterns such as repetition. Moreover, since the updated student in turn defines the rollout policy of subsequent rounds, these patterns persist and potentially amplify over training. This failure mode suggests that improving OPD requires treating the rollout policy itself as an optimization target, rather than only modifying the distillation objective. We propose Mixture Proposal On-Policy Distillation (MPOPD), which derives a student-anchored, teacher-guided rollout distribution from a local trust-region objective based on relative teacher support. The resulting target is a geometric mixture of the student and teacher policies, and we introduce a proposal policy that approximates this mixture, enabling rollout generation in a single forward pass. Experiments on 16 math, knowledge, and medical reasoning benchmarks show consistent gains over OPD and recent variants. On math reasoning, our method improves average accuracy by 6.91 over OPD on Qwen3-4B-Base while reducing average generation length by 41%, suggesting that rollout policy design provides an effective way to improve OPD.

##### **LM-DSB: A Lightweight Multi-agent System for Automated Daily Safety Bulletin Generation in Construction**
2610.36433v1 by Xianxiang Zhao, Advik Mehta, Falak Sethi, Brian Gue, Qipei Mei, Lingzi Wu

Construction safety management still struggles to produce daily safety bulletins (DSBs), structured daily communications that identify site-specific hazards, convey safety protocols, and set preventive measures for workers. Manual DSB creation takes 2-3 hours daily and yields inconsistent quality, with 25% of crews never conducting pretask planning (Olson et al., 2016), diverting safety personnel from field supervision. This study proposes the Lightweight Multi-Agent DSB System (LM-DSB), which integrates retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) with a locally deployable Large Language Model (LLM) to automate DSB generation on consumer-grade personal computers. LM-DSB coordinates specialized planner, researcher, and writer agents in a hierarchical architecture, enhanced by domain-specific fine-tuning on construction safety documentation and RAG grounding in 37 health, safety, and environment (HSE) documents. A two-phase validation assessed feasibility. Phase 1 comparative evaluation showed the multi-agent RAG system (Llama 3-8B) outperformed all alternative configurations, with strong lexical alignment and coherence (TF-IDF: 0.70, BLEU: 0.39, ROUGE-1: 0.66) and near-complete document generation (length ratio: 1.01 vs. 0.39 for single-agent RAG). Phase 2 expert evaluation (N=40) confirmed practical acceptability (mean scores 3.50-4.20 on a 5-point scale), particularly in regulatory compliance (4.20) and factual accuracy (4.10). The findings show that architectural design enables lightweight models to handle safety-critical documentation on consumer-grade hardware, advancing technology democratization in construction safety management. Limitations include knowledge base scope, language constraints, and lack of field deployment validation; future work should address multimodal integration and workflow adoption.

##### **Reliability-Aware Short-Term Glycemic Risk Prediction Using an Anchor-Based Comparative Framework of Sequence, Multimodal, and State-Updating Digital-Twin Models**
2610.36363v1 by Pir Bakhsh Khokhar, Carmine Gravino, Fabio Palomba

Anticipating short-term hypoglycemic and hyperglycemic risk is essential for proactive diabetes management. However, existing machine learning approaches often use inconsistent evaluation protocols and emphasize predictive performance over reliability. This study introduces an anchor-based benchmarking framework integrating longitudinal glucose sequences and multimodal patient information. Using the T1DiabetesGranada dataset, 5,503,441 standardized prediction anchors were constructed from 736 individuals with type 1 diabetes to evaluate hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia prediction at 30-, 60-, and 120-minute horizons. Three neural architectures, a Hybrid LSTM-Transformer, a Multimodal Digital Twin, and a State-Updating Digital Twin with gap-aware longitudinal state progression, were compared under identical patient-level validation protocols. Beyond predictive performance, the evaluation examined calibration, classification thresholds, explanation reliability, cross-model disagreement, and robustness to controlled perturbations. No statistically significant predictive differences were observed after Holm correction, although the models exhibited complementary reliability characteristics. The Multimodal Digital Twin achieved the lowest mean Brier score, while the State-Updating Digital Twin demonstrated the lowest mean expected calibration error (ECE-10). The Hybrid LSTM-Transformer showed strong explanation faithfulness and stability under its temporal attribution protocol. These findings highlight the importance of evaluating digital-twin models beyond predictive accuracy and emphasize the need for reliability assessment and external validation before clinical deployment.

##### **Enhancing Clinical Classifier Robustness with Deep Representation Learning Based OOD Detection on DNA Methylation Data**
2610.36344v1 by Md Zahangir Alom, Quynh T. Tran, Breuer Alexandar, Brent A. Orr

DNA methylation (DNAM) array-based tumor classification is a powerful clinical diagnostic tool, but models trained on fixed reference cohorts can produce faulty predictions for tumor types not represented in the training distribution. Deep neural network (DNN) classifiers were trained on public sarcoma (SARC) (GSE140686) and brain tumor (BT) (GSE90496) cohorts, and feature vectors were extracted from bottleneck layers. Five OOD methods, including 1dimension (1D)-Subspace Analysis (1D-SSA), K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN), Ridge Regression, Mahalanobis Distance (MD), and Sum of Squared Distances (SSD), were explored with 200 and 500-dimensional embeddings. Spectral discrepancy was used to discriminate in-distribution (IND) from OOD samples. In the sarcoma-IND/brain tumor-OOD setting, the framework achieved an AUROC of 0.975 and True Negative Rate (TNR)@95TPR of 0.930, and an AUROC of 0.9569 and TNR@95TPR of 0.8660 for the brain tumor-IND/sarcoma-OOD setting. On the independent clinical test set, the method yielded a mean AUROC of 0.976, a DE of 0.032, an FPR@95TPR of 0.065, and a TNR@95TPR of 0.935. ##### **OA-RAG: Ontology-based Agentic RAG for Faithful LLM Reasoning**
2610.36335v1 by Hui Yang, Jiaoyan Chen, Yongsheng Gao

Large language models (LLMs) perform well across many real-world tasks but remain prone to hallucination, limiting their reliability in domains such as biomedicine and healthcare, where answers must be grounded in established knowledge. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) mitigates this problem by incorporating external information, but often overlooks logical relationships and relies heavily on the LLM's own reasoning, which can compromise answer faithfulness. We propose OA-RAG, an agent that integrates formal reasoning with LLM reasoning, drawing on both the logical structure and informal annotations of domain ontologies. For each answer, OA-RAG reports the ontology observations used as evidence and a model-assessed grounding level (entailed, supported, related, or unrelated) that characterises the strength of that evidence. Across benchmarks derived from three large-scale ontologies and six real-world medical question-answering tasks, OA-RAG achieves the best overall performance among the evaluated ontology-fine-tuned and popular RAG methods, with particularly strong results on reasoning-intensive tasks. The reported grounding levels also offer a useful indication of answer reliability, with stronger grounding generally associated with higher accuracy. Finally, our results demonstrate the value of ontologies as knowledge sources: OA-RAG is competitive with, and sometimes outperforms, domain-specific RAG systems that draw on sources such as textbooks.

##### **Ontology-guided multi-agent reasoning for explainable differential diagnosis**
2610.36325v1 by Roxana Ramona Szomiu, George Ursu, Adrian Groza

Effective differential diagnosis depends on well structured and unambiguous patient case descriptions, since incomplete or ambiguous clinical information readily leads to diagnostic errors and inconsistent reasoning. Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently shown promising capabilities in medical question answering and clinical reasoning; however, even when achieving competitive performance on medical benchmarks, they often rely on pattern recognition learned from training data rather than grounded clinical evidence, and can generate plausible yet unsupported diagnostic conclusions. Their reasoning remains largely opaque, making it difficult for clinicians to assess the evidence and rationale behind a given recommendation. To address this gap, we propose an ontology-guided, multiagent differential diagnosis framework that replicates the iterative reasoning process of physicians. The framework grounds diagnostic inference in a structured, expert-curated medical knowledge base, enabling interpretation of symptoms, clinical findings, or disease relationships, while decomposing diagnosis into explicit, auditable reasoning steps. This ontology-based grounding constrains inference to evidence-backed associations, reducing hallucination and improving diagnostic transparency, traceability, and consistency. The system then identifies the most discriminative missing symptoms and generates targeted follow-up questions to reduce uncertainty through a process that mirrors to clinical inquiry. Together, these components improve the reliability, explainability, and diagnostic accuracy of AI-assisted differential diagnosis. The source code is publicly available.

##### **CohortFlow: A Steerable Clinical Cohort Curation Agent with Localized Semantic Correction**
2610.36100v1 by Neil Chen, Adrian Serapio, Hanxue Gu, Evan Lee, Kang Wang, Yang Yang

Language model agents for clinical cohort curation should accommodate evolving eligibility criteria without repeatedly processing unchanged requirements. We introduce CohortFlow, a steerable agent that represents natural language corrections as localized updates to a structured cohort specification and reconciles them with a persistent execution graph. Explicitly recorded semantic dependencies enable affected computations to be selectively invalidated while preserving valid prior results. On 100 scenarios derived from clinical-trial eligibility criteria, comprising 126 correction turns, localized steering achieves 77.0% requirement micro-F1 versus 76.4% for full regeneration using the same 8B model, a small numerical advantage that is not statistically significant ($p=0.493$). It reduces mean correction latency by 64.8% after completion and 44.3% during execution, with 38.1% fewer model calls. In a separate proof-of-concept evaluation over MIMIC, all 13 cohort states execute successfully, while correction trajectories demonstrate selective preservation of unaffected computations and recomputation of changed or dependent nodes. Together, these results show that localized semantic steering can substantially reduce correction latency and model calls while maintaining strong requirement-level accuracy.

2610.35761v1 by Sudeshna Jana, Siddhartha Laha, Manjira Sinha, Tirthankar Dasgupta, Pabitra Mitra

Mental-health risks such as depression, dementia, and suicidal ideation can emerge during hospitalization among chronically ill patients and may worsen without timely recognition and intervention. However, most existing mental health studies focus on patients with established psychiatric conditions rather than patients hospitalized primarily for physical illness. In this work, we investigate whether latent mental-health risk in chronically ill patients can be inferred from longitudinal clinical narratives. We construct a weakly supervised cohort around 4,000 patients from MIMIC-III using mental-health evidence extracted from discharge summaries. ##### **The Watchtower Imperative: Lifespan-Calibrated Evaluation for Frontier AI**
2610.35725v1 by Casey M. Williams, Jennifer Victoria Scurrell

The gap between AI capability deployment and safety assurance is structural and cannot be closed through internal evaluations alone. Analyzing documented harms (2017-2026) across the human lifespan reveals that disparate incidents --- from voice assistants endangering children to AI companions implicated in adolescent suicide and automated care denials for the elderly --- reflect a single failure: evaluating systems against an implicit adult user model. We introduce five developmental moderators structured into a Lifespan Vulnerability Matrix across six life stages. The matrix reveals a nonmonotonic risk distribution: vulnerability peaks at both ends of the lifespan, governance coverage is asymmetrical, and authority to convert model errors into real-world impact is scrutinized least. We then propose a set of lifespan design principles generalizing existing child-protective instruments, and an institution-agnostic, three-tier external evaluation framework --- comprising automated simulation, expert red teaming, and post-deployment monitoring --- indexed directly to matrix risk cells.

#### Abstracts
##### **MDSKILL: Learning to Internalize Skill Memory through Multi-View Distillation for Efficient Dynamic Agent Self-Evolution**
2611.05313v1 by Dian Jin, Jinluan Yang, Kairong Han, Baohong Li, Zijing Hu, Yi He, Wenlin Zhong, Yunze Tong, Minqin Zhu, Linchuan Li, Yanan Niu, Kun Kuang

Skill memories encode past experiences as textual prompts to improve reinforcement learning (RL) for self-evolving agents. Complementing RL, On-Policy Self-Distillation (OPSD) provides dense, fine-grained supervision to internalize knowledge from skill-guided trajectories and accelerate policy learning. However, self-generated memories often contain noisy supervision and functionally entangled, redundant, or conflicting knowledge, making direct distillation difficult. Under limited training budgets, these issues can hinder knowledge internalization and even degrade performance. To address this problem, we propose MDSKILL, a multi-view distillation approach for skill memory internalization. MDSKILL organizes skill memories into three complementary functional views (state grounding, action planning, and execution control) and selects high-quality skills using dynamic metrics to construct view-specific and integrated reasoning branches. OPSD then transfers and consolidates complementary experiential knowledge across these branches into a shared policy, enabling efficient skill internalization and continuous agent self-evolution. We evaluate MDSKILL on three agent benchmarks against RL, skill-augmented, and self-distillation baselines that do not rely on stronger external teacher models. Without additional computational overhead, MDSKILL improves upon SOTA performance by up to 7.1% and 8.1% on ALFWorld and WebShop. Extensive ablations and evolution analyses further validate its effectiveness in knowledge internalization and policy improvement. The code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/MDSKILL-CF53.

摘要：

##### **Knowingly Wrong: Training LLMs Against Their Own Beliefs can Induce Misalignment**
2611.05287v1 by Ariana M. Villegas-Suarez, Guido Freire, Sohan Venkatesh, Bartosz Cywiński

Fine-tuning a large language model (LLM) on harmful data in a narrow domain, such as bad medical advice, can make it broadly misaligned. How realistic this risk is remains unclear, since models are very rarely fine-tuned on explicitly harmful data in practice. In this work, we show that fine-tuning on data the model believes to be false but that has no harmful content, can also induce broad misalignment. Across mathematics, trivia, cooking and sports, the more strongly the model disbelieves the answers it is trained on, the more misaligned it becomes. In mathematics, misaligned answers rise from 4% to over 60% as the training answers become more wrong, with little loss of general capability. After fine-tuning, the model states the wrong answers but its multiple-choice probabilities still prefer the true ones, which suggests that misalignment comes from this internal inconsistency. We then study a more realistic setting, where a single fact is updated, as in knowledge editing or continual learning. The same relationship holds and the more the model still believes the true fact after training on the false one, the more misaligned it becomes. When we frame the same false fact as part of a coherent story, the model comes to believe it more deeply and becomes less misaligned than when we instruct it to assert the false fact. Content filters and persona vectors cannot tell these two framings apart, even though they lead to different levels of misalignment. These results suggest a risk for model updates, where training a model that does not adopt the new fact as true can induce misalignment even when the data looks harmless.

摘要：

##### **The Geometry of Ignorance: Detecting Suppressed Knowledge in LLMs through Activation Fragmentation**
2611.05272v1 by Adar Peleg, Dvir Alsheich, Tomer Ashuach, Avi Mendelson

Evaluating whether a large language model (LLM) genuinely knows a concept remains a fundamental problem, since behavioral metrics can be fooled by suppression: a model that refuses to answer may still harbor recoverable knowledge. The framework measures the geometric coherence of \textit{anon-vectors}, the differences in hidden states between a concept-specific query and its anonymized paraphrase, and shows that genuine knowledge forms a tight, low-entropy cluster while ignorance forces geometric fragmentation. Across ten instruction-tuned LLMs from three model families, all fragmentation metrics reliably separate known from unknown concepts, and post-unlearning, fragmentation geometry correlates with adversarial recovery across four attack types and five unlearning algorithms, though the direction of that relationship is shown to depend on attack methodology. Furthermore, a layer-resolved analysis reveals that different adversarial mechanisms exploit this geometry at varying depths, with high attack-specific correlations that are invisible to behavioral evaluation. Together, these results constitute a single-phase, attack-free proxy for unlearning robustness that captures aspects of parametric knowledge inaccessible to output-level metrics. A reference implementation is available at https://github.com/AdarPeleg/geometry-of-ignorance

摘要：

##### **OperAItor: An Agentic Framework for Fault-Tolerant Operation of Chemical Processes**
2611.05179v1 by Javal Vyas, Mehmet Mercang{ö}z

OperAItor, an open-source framework for developing and evaluating large language model agents for supervisory fault management in chemical processes. The framework is demonstrated on a dynamic reactor-distillation process representing isopropanol dehydrogenation, with physically based thermodynamics and modified reaction kinetics to permit liquid-phase operation. The process is represented by a 113-variable differential-algebraic model and operates at a nominal feed rate of 100kmol/h, producing approximately 4t/h of acetone. The simulated plant includes 8 PI control loops, 22 process alarms, 5 duty-standby pump trains, and 11 independent safety instrumented functions. The agent architecture integrates multivariate statistical process monitoring, fault diagnosis, knowledge-graph retrieval, action planning, and supervisory action generation. Five action-validation configurations are investigated, comprising operation without validation, an LLM-based judge, digital-twin simulation, and deterministic admissibility checks with and without consequence projection. The framework is evaluated using four LLMs across 20 scenarios involving single and multiple simultaneous faults. Without agent intervention, 18 scenarios result in plant stops, corresponding to an economic loss of $399,000 relative to fault-free operation under the assumed benchmark economics. For GPT-5 nano, Qwen3 8B, and Mistral 7B, validated agent configurations reduce the number of stops to 5-8 and recover $265,000 of the loss. The study provides a reproducible benchmark for comparing agent architectures, language models, and action-validation methods for supervisory fault management in chemical processes.

摘要：

##### **AgentAM: An AI Agent for Spatter- and Defect-Aware Process Optimization in Laser Powder Bed Fusion**
2611.05171v1 by Olabode T. Ajenifujah, Amir Barati Farimani

Predictive models for laser powder bed fusion (LPBF) are rarely used on the shop floor because querying them requires programming and model-specific knowledge. We present AgentAM, a large language model (LLM) agent that gives process engineers natural-language access to CFD-trained surrogates of spatter and melt pool geometry for SS316L through the Model Context Protocol (MCP). Polynomial surrogates trained on 279 single-track simulations predict melt pool dimensions ($R^2 \geq 0.95$) and spatter volume ($R^2 = 0.83$ on test data) at any power-velocity point in the trained range, and geometric criteria assign each point a geometry-derived defect regime (keyhole, lack of fusion, balling or stable) that matches the regime obtained from the simulated geometry in 93.4% of tracks under cross-validation. The agent selects and parameterizes tools for prediction, process-window scans and constrained optimization. Minimum-spatter optima often sat within 10 $μ$m of the lack-of-fusion boundary; a margin-aware optimizer moved them away from all thresholds and raised the estimated probability of remaining classified Good from 0.94 to 1.00 under an assumed 2% process drift, and from 0.72 to 0.91 when jointly sampled cross-validated geometry residuals were added. About 40-50 well-spread simulations sufficed to reproduce the defect map. To evaluate the agent layer, we introduce a 145-prompt benchmark scored against direct tool calls, plus eight prompts (40 runs) on margin-aware requests. Over the 725 runs of the main benchmark the agent passed the intended inputs in 98.5% of parsing prompts, reported the scored spatter values and defect classes faithfully and met every stated constraint in the 65 feasible optimization runs, within a median 1.0% of the brute-force optimum.

摘要：

##### **Meta$^2$: Precomputing Reusable Knowledge for Online Harness Evolution in Long-Horizon Agents**
2611.05139v1 by Lingzhi Yuan, Yiguang Cai, Weize Liu, Siqi Yang, Feijiang Han, Yongkyun Lee, Udari Madhushani Sehwag, Furong Huang

Harness optimization improves long-horizon agents by refining the prompts, tools, memory, and hooks around a frozen model. Offline methods can learn from completed trajectories, but deploy one shared harness that may overfit training tasks and cannot react to evidence revealed during a new run. Online evolution can adapt before, during, or after one run through an evolver, but may have to reason about every intervention from scratch or rely on verifier feedback that is usually unavailable at test time. We introduce Meta$^2$, which moves offline optimization one level up: rather than optimizing a shared harness, it learns reusable knowledge for harness evolution and applies it within each task online. Offline, Meta$^2$ learns a textual Meta-Skill $M$ by contrasting training rollouts of the same task agent under three configurations: without an evolver, with an evolver guided by the current version of $M$, and with an evolver given privileged verifier information available only in training. Contrasts in trajectories and outcomes are aggregated across tasks to refine $M$ into reusable guidance on when and how to evolve, when to leave the harness unchanged, and which interventions to avoid. Online, an evolver combines the frozen $M$ with emerging trajectory evidence to decide whether to KEEP the harness unchanged or EVOLVE it through a task-local edit. Each edit applies to the rest of the same run without resetting its environment or progress. Thus, evolution guidance transfers across tasks while harness edits remain task-local, and held-out verifier feedback is never available to the evolver. On two long-horizon benchmarks, Terminal-Bench 2.1 and DeepSWE 1.1, Meta$^2$ achieves the highest performance among evaluated methods with GPT-5.6-Terra, reaching 81.00% and 44.44%, respectively. Ablations support the value of contrastive evolution guidance and keeping online harness edits task-local.

摘要：

##### **Workload-Static Optical Circuit Switching For Scalable LLM Inference Decode**
2611.05104v1 by Maximilian Thürmer, Nicolas Scotto di Perto, Eloise Stein, Natanijel Vasic, Mikaeil Bagherli, Zhenguo Wu, Benjamin Klenk, Holger Fröning, Johannes Feldmann

LLM inference decode executes fine-grained collectives whose latency directly limits per-user interactivity, yet scale-up fabrics are still built from electronic packet switches designed for training. We observe that, for a given model deployment and parallelization, the communication structure of decode is fixed, and propose workload-static optical circuit switching (OCS): circuits are configured once per deployment, co-designed with model parallelism and with topology-aware collective schedules that turn spare accelerator ports into fewer communication phases or higher bandwidth. We evaluate this design with GXNET, a graph-based workload emulator coupled to analytical and flit-level network models grounded in hardware measurements. In a 576-accelerator scale-up domain serving 5T-parameter MoE models with up to 5M tokens of context, all-OCS fabrics improve interactivity by up to 65% and energy efficiency by up to 119% over 4:1 oversubscribed packet-switched fabrics.

摘要：

##### **ToolSpace: Scaling Tool-Centric Environment Synthesis for Long-Horizon Agents**
2611.05036v1 by Guanting Dong, Zhichao Sun, Xinyu Yang, Rui Wang, Xin Deng, Jiajie Jin, Zhixiong Yang, Junhao Huang, Han Han, Mingyu Ma, Xiaotao Wang, Yuyang Hu, Xiaoshuai Song, Xiaoxi Li, Jiejun Tan, Zhecan Wang, Shihan Dou, Honglin Guo, Zhiheng Xi, Tao Gui, Tao Ji, Qi Zhang, Yutao Zhu, Ji-Rong Wen, Zhicheng Dou

Large language models are deployed as long-horizon agents that compose diverse tools across MCP services into executable workflows. Existing MCP environment-scaling methods are data-centric: they first synthesize a database and scenario, then derive tools and tasks from them. As a result, tool interfaces may not match real ones, tool compositions are not grounded in execution, and tasks rarely span more than one environment. We propose ToolSpace, a tool-centric framework that reverses this order. ToolSpace mines data, state, and retry dependencies from adjacent calls in execution traces of real MCP services and open agent corpora, using deterministic rules rather than LLM judgment, and clusters the resulting weighted tool-composition graph into environments. Databases are synthesized only for state-mutating environments, while query and stateless tools replay recorded returns. ToolSpace then performs state-aware random walks with rollback and decompiles each chain into a natural-language task with assertion-based checks, keeping only samples that replay successfully from the initial state. Applied to AutomationBench and tau3-Bench Banking, the pipeline produces harder Pro versions on which 7 frontier models drop by up to 38.2 points. Across seven long-horizon tool-use benchmarks, ToolSpace raises Qwen3.6-35B-A3B from 23.0 to 41.0 average score (43.2 with RL) and GLM-5.2 from 44.6 to 56.6, on par with Claude Opus 5. Ablations show that execution-witnessed edges clearly outperform LLM-judged ones, and performance keeps improving with data and environment scale.

We construct 250 questions, with 50 each from Biology, Chemistry, Medical Imaging, Mechanical CAD, and Architecture, through a pipeline combining synthetic question generation with domain-expert review and annotation. Each task pairs a native 3D artifact with a scientific question, a specified response format, and a task-specific reference answer, requiring domain knowledge to be applied to geometric or topological evidence. We evaluate 12 models on the same questions in three settings: visual question answering with native 3D artifacts accessed through an agent harness's file and code-execution tools; the same workflow augmented with expert-inspired viewer inspection and measurement tools exposed through the Model Context Protocol (MCP); and visual question answering from prescribed rendered 2D images using an agent harness. All settings use the same task-specific deterministic final-answer scoring. The inspection environment supports view changes, target isolation, and slice inspection. In this main comparison, the best mean answer score is 0.53 out of 1. Mean scores in the native-artifact workflow without additional MCP tools exceed those in the rendered-image workflow for every model, by 17.2 percentage points on average.

##### **Query-based Submodular Data Selection for Efficient Instruction Tuning**
2611.04059v1 by Zhenghao Zhao, Yasutomo Kawanishi, Huihuo Zheng, Michael E. Papka, Yan Yan

Given a large heterogeneous training pool and a small target set specifying the desired capability, targeted data selection aims to find a compact subset that maximizes downstream performance. Existing methods score each candidate independently against the target and select the top-ranked examples, yet this point-wise strategy ignores inter-example interactions: at tight budgets the selected subset is dominated by near-redundant points that waste capacity on overlapping information. The resulting objective balances target relevance and intra-subset redundancy using the same formulation across different selection budgets. Empirically, the benefit of diversity-aware selection becomes increasingly pronounced as the data budget decreases. To further close coverage gaps that no selection from existing data can bridge, we introduce a gradient-guided synthesis step that identifies under-covered target directions via residual subspace analysis and generates synthetic examples to fill them. Experiments on MMLU, BBH, and TydiQA across Llama-2, Llama-3, and Mistral, show that QSGS at a 0.5% budget matches or exceeds full-data performance on Mistral-7B, outperforms the strongest baseline across all benchmarks, and yields increasing gains as the budget shrinks.

##### **What makes a theorem interesting? Operationalizing interestingness for open-ended exploration**
2611.02905v1 by Silvère Gangloff

An agent that explores, whether it generates conjectures or research ideas, must decide which of them are worth keeping. This judgment of interestingness is usually delegated to a hand-picked proxy or to a language model, neither of which is known to correspond to what humans actually value. I study it for mathematical theorems, by observing which results mathematicians single out in formal libraries and in papers. I model each way of singling out results as an evaluator that weights features of a result's statement, its proof and dependencies, and split its weights into a shared and a subjective part. In formalized mathematics, ex-ante features predict these selections as well as a large language-model judge, and the two complement each other. Interestingness is also largely shared. One set of weights, carried mostly by structural features such as the length of the statement and of the proof and the subfields the proof draws on, accounts for most of the signal and transfers in part across formal libraries. What remains subjective depends on practices and individual authors more than on subfields. The judge's advantage lies in its knowledge of the canon, and neither the judge nor the features rely on memory of the papers. The approach, which infers what a community finds interesting from what it singles out, can be applied to other sciences.

Dynamic expectations and new observations jointly inform the current estimate, which in turn anchors personalized forecasts based on shared knowledge and individual experience. ##### **FactorSpec: Serving Exact Relational Constraints as Speculative Drafts**
2611.02297v1 by Jianchang Su, Dawei Xiang, Wei Zhang

Speculative decoding speeds up LLM serving by drafting tokens that the target model verifies in one pass, and its gain grows with the drafts the target accepts. Structured outputs should be an ideal case, because much of a record follows from relations among its fields: an itinerary's days follow from its cities' stays and flights. Serving engines, however, draft such text by proposing it from the prompt or a learned head, and their grammar masks enforce only syntax, so the model must satisfy the relations, which it violates: on NATURAL PLAN's trip problems, Qwen3-8B writes a valid itinerary for 10%, and retries that name the violated relation raise this to 14%. We present FactorSpec, which serves exact relational constraints as speculative drafts. FactorSpec compiles a JSON Schema and its relations into a factor graph with an exact feasibility check, drafts every token the constraint determines as certain, verifies each request at a width chosen for it, and computes the masks in worker processes outside the scheduler while the GPU runs. In SGLang, its drafts yield up to 10.5 tokens per verify step, up to 1.9x those of the fastest n-gram drafter, and it decodes up to 1.43x faster than speculators tuned per load, at a grammar mask's cost; it also leads in vLLM. Because every record is also valid, it serves up to 13.9x the valid records per GPU-second of syntax-only baselines.

The effectiveness of such systems depends not only on large language model capabilities, but also on the readiness of heterogeneous data environments across the Architecture, Engineering, and Construction (AEC) sector. These environments combine structured sources, such as CAD and BIM models, schedules, cost databases, and asset registers, with unstructured ones, including technical reports, design narratives, planning and policy documents, contracts, meeting minutes, and risk registers. This paper reviews how built environment data can be organized to support agentic AI-ready workflows, focusing on knowledge structures, interoperability, and access and integration mechanisms such as retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and the Model Context Protocol (MCP). The paper synthesizes current perspectives, highlights challenges around fragmentation and interoperability, and identifies open questions for future research on AI-compatible information management in the built environment.

##### **When the Text Is Silent: Memory Shortcuts and the Validity of LLM Annotation under Evidence Insufficiency**
2611.02273v1 by Wenchang Wang, Yue Song

We call this behavior a memory shortcut and examine when it emerges, what amplifies it, and how to mitigate it. Using 995 widely recognized public figures and four matched control names per figure, we construct counterfactual texts that separate textual evidence from prior knowledge, and evaluate three commercial LLMs on 121,594 prompts each across five stages. We find a striking asymmetry. Although all models recognize nearly every figure, none follows memory over the text when the two conflict. Shortcuts instead emerge when evidence is absent: for one model, famous names raise the rate of remembered occupations, ages, and countries by 17.0, 14.9, and 6.8 percentage points relative to synthetic names. These effects occur only for recognized figures, and 98.6% of the resulting labels are factually correct about the namesake while none is supported by the text. ##### **Who Should Score the Cache? Mid-Depth Layers Are the Best Judges of KV-Cache Eviction**
2611.02230v1 by Ekaterina Seregina

Key-value (KV) cache eviction methods rank tokens by attention scores, and each layer scores its own cache. To our knowledge, no eviction method asks which layer should score. On the eighteen models with per-layer caches, spanning 0.5B to 109B, long-context retrieval accuracy collapses at shallow layers, peaks between 0.4 and 0.75 of depth, and degrades at the last layer. The curve arises because a layer judges tokens well only once it has used the question to find the answer, which takes several layers. Modeling eviction as a top-$k$ cut, we compute answer survival probability from prefill attention and reproduce the measured curves. This approach selects the scoring layer using calibration prompts with known answers, and the selected layer is the best layer measured on most models and never significantly below it. It improves on the own-layer default on sixteen of the eighteen models, with significant gains on seven, all at 14B or below, while on larger models the default is already close to the best mid-depth layer. Scoring every cache from the selected layer while keeping one set of tokens for all heads also beats per-head SnapKV by 18 to 43 points on five models up to 14B under tight budgets. The same curve explains a penalty in shared-cache models, where several layers read one cache and the layer that writes it scores by default. We then develop an automatic inference engine whose input-conditioned background theories address lexical relations, quantification, quantities, event structure and temporal dependencies. Prover9 supplies proofs for entailment and contradiction, while Mace4 supports bounded consistency checks. Worked constructions, axiom schemas and proof analyses explain these outcomes and identify the contributions of scope, reference, lexical knowledge and numerical structure. ##### **Order-Only On-Policy Distillation**
2611.02112v1 by Guangzeng Han, Yu Wu, Xiaolei Huang

On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own generations by matching the teacher's token probabilities. These probabilities entangle two kinds of information: which tokens the teacher prefers, and how confident it is. The latter reflects the teacher's own capacity and calibration, which a smaller student may be unable to reproduce. Because the teacher temperature changes these values without changing the ordering, OPD is also sensitive to the temperature choice. We ask whether the teacher's ordering of candidate tokens is enough, and propose Order-Only On-Policy Distillation (O$^{3}$PD). At each student-generated prefix, O$^{3}$PD trains the student to reproduce the order of the teacher's top-$K$ tokens, without fitting their probability values. The objective is therefore invariant to the teacher temperature and needs no temperature tuning. Across six teacher--student pairs, O$^{3}$PD outperforms OPD on the average over HMMT25, AIME24, and AIME25 in every pair, by up to 2.9 points, with gains of up to 5.6 points on AIME25. Other ranking losses yield similar gains, so the benefit comes from ordinal supervision itself. Analysis shows that O$^{3}$PD departs further from the teacher's probabilities than OPD, yet reproduces its ranking more faithfully and yields a stronger student. The ranking, not the probability values, thus carries the knowledge that on-policy distillation needs to transfer.

##### **Do Embodied VLMs Actually Use Their Scene-Graph Memory?**
2611.02056v1 by Shubh Garg, Divyansh Pandey, Vedant Khandelwal, Niyati Rawal, Amit Sheth

Embodied agents that answer questions about their route need a memory of what they saw, and scene graphs are a natural form for it. A vision-language model (VLM) can receive such a graph as text or through a graph encoder, but accuracy alone cannot show whether the graph holds the episode's facts, reaches the model, or is actually relied on. In this diagnostic study, we introduce a graph-swap test for the last of these: each episode is given another episode's graph, and we count how many answers change. On an embodied question-answering benchmark with routes through two to seven rooms, we use it to compare ways of delivering scene-graph memory to a fine-tuned VLM. As text, the memory is used strongly, adding 30 points on route questions. As a graph, it is not, and we trace why: the encoder preserves the information, but standard pooled and late fusion discard it. Giving each node its own input token recovers part of it, a gain of 8.3 points on route questions that the swap test confirms comes from the graph. The test also exposes a shortcut that accuracy hides: when the same facts appear in the text, the model ignores its graph. The test thus offers a cheap check of whether a VLM uses its scene-graph memory, and our analysis points to how that memory should be delivered.

##### **Hippocampal consolidation without replay in fully fine-tuned networks**
2611.01952v1 by Keuntae Kim, Yong Suk Choi

Networks that keep learning overwrite what they know. Prevailing remedies replay stored data, distil from old models or freeze the backbone. We show that this division lets a fully trainable network keep learning without cross-task replay, a teacher or inference-time memory. After each task, a training-only hippocampal memory indexes the input geometry of every group of synapses sharing an input, residual energy included, and limits each synapse's movement per direction before and after the optimizer. To our knowledge, this is the first demonstration of one principle keeping whole networks learning across four architecture families and models up to 8 billion parameters. Methods that freeze the backbone hold up only as far as its representation already separates the classes; here the whole network keeps learning to tell near-duplicates apart, leading the frozen-backbone baseline by 17.5 points on TACRED. Geometry is the very form that connects space, time, and matter, serving as the fundamental fabric of the physical world, from continuous fields to discrete structures. 2611.01893v1 by Haotian Wu, Xu Ji, Qiheng Li, Li Xiong, Zibin Pan

Domain skew challenges federated learning by requiring a shared model to accommodate distinct visual distributions. Domain-specific experts can capture these distributions separately, creating an opportunity to integrate their representations in a unified predictor. We propose Federated Knowledge-Preserving Learning (FKPL), a two-stage framework that combines domain-oriented expert training with prototype-based classifier calibration. First, a one-shot subspace-based clustering procedure groups clients for intra-cluster training. The resulting expert encoders are concatenated and frozen, preserving each learned feature mapping during global integration. Second, clients interpolate their concatenated features with class-matched domain prototypes and upload the resulting virtual features. The server trains a global linear classifier on these features, concentrating classifier optimization at the server after a single feature upload. FKPL achieves average accuracies of 73.01\%, 67.00\%, and 64.51\% on Office-Caltech-10, PACS, and a four-domain subset of DomainNet, respectively. On DomainNet, its cross-domain standard deviation is 7.50 percentage points, the lowest among the evaluated methods. With the same concatenated extractor, prototype-based calibration improves average accuracy over federated classifier training by 9.99 and 14.94 percentage points on Office-Caltech-10 and PACS. These results identify classifier calibration as an effective way to combine preserved domain representations into a global predictor.

摘要：

##### **Don't Guess, Ask Wisely: Benchmarking and Training Proactive Elicitation in Open-Domain LLM Conversations**
2611.01878v1 by Rongyu Zhang, Mutian Bao, Jinjian Zhang, Yuetian Du, He Xu, Wei Zhou, Xiaojun Zhu, Ming Kong, Jie Liu, Linjian Mo, Qiang Zhu

When user queries are incomplete, large language models should ask targeted questions before committing to an answer, a capability we call proactive elicitation, yet evaluation on real user-assistant queries across seven LLMs (8B to frontier) shows that fewer than 11% of underspecified queries trigger a follow-up question. To benchmark and train this capability, we introduce VagueBench, where each seed query is annotated with structured intent and detail units then degraded into intent-vague and fully-vague variants via controlled removal of linguistic cues, yielding known hidden information sets for evaluating both when and what to ask. We propose DialogGRPO, an offline reinforcement learning method that organizes multi-turn elicitation as a dialogue-state directed acyclic graph, where candidate questions at each node receive dense turn-level rewards combining immediate information gain with lookahead value through group-relative comparison, enabling fine-grained credit assignment without runtime simulation. DialogGRPO elicits missing information on over 88% of vague queries with substantial gains in both recovery efficiency and answer coverage, without over-asking on complete queries, outperforms prompting and test-time-scaling baselines, and generalizes zero-shot to MultiWOZ 2.4 and AmbigQA.

摘要：

##### **CoGSeq-LTV: Scalable Missing-Aware Graph-Sequence Modeling for Multi-Business Transaction Analytics**
2611.01817v1 by Ziyang Su, Yao Zhou, Kelin Liu, Guanjie Zheng, Linghe Kong

Large-scale super-apps generate heterogeneous transaction logs in which users interact with multiple businesses, item identifiers are only partially available, and future value targets are sparse, heavy-tailed, and cumulative over time. We study multi-business, multi-horizon value prediction as a data-intensive analytics problem over such logs, rather than as a single-table task. (i) a batch-induced user-item graph that provides collaborative signals without materializing a global graph and remains applicable when item identifiers are missing through reserved unknown-item handling, (ii) business-aware sequence encoders that preserve recency, inter-event gaps, payment context, and goods-table availability, and (iii) cross-view contrastive alignment that transfers user-level intent across businesses while retaining business-specific temporal patterns. To handle zero-inflated and long-tailed cumulative targets, CoGSeq-LTV adopts a zero-inflated mixture log-normal head with MAE-aligned point supervision and validation-calibrated zero-aware inference. Experiments on the BiBiSheng transaction-log dataset show that CoGSeq-LTV improves over representative LTV baselines across businesses and horizons, including under a later out-of-time evaluation window.

摘要：

##### **Less but Sufficient: Relation Knowledge Selects Compact Knowledge-Graph Contexts, and What External Checks Can and Cannot Catch**
2611.01733v1 by Seunghui Jwa, Minsu Oh, Sangwoo Hong, Yeo Chan Yoon

Large language models (LLMs) read knowledge-graph facts ranked by relevance, leaving open whether they suffice or conflict. Our checks use relation knowledge, written by hand or learned from training questions labelled with their relation paths. It selects a compact context of facts yielding an answer, and derives it; a derivability check tests whether an answer follows, a consistency check whether facts conflict, and a validation-chosen routing gate decides where the derived answer replaces the LLM's. Seven open LLMs read the compact context better in exact match than relevance-ranked facts; on ComplexWebQuestions (CWQ) they match SubgraphRAG's facts at a quarter of the prompt tokens, and stronger LLMs read SubgraphRAG's better. Where the routing gate passes, ours leads SubgraphRAG's in exact match in all 53 LLM, benchmark and prompt settings. A precision gate, returning the derived answer only when the check passes with exactly one derived entity, answered 18 and 5 percent of held-out WebQSP and CWQ questions in a pre-specified test, right for 90 and 96 percent. The consistency check judges facts by path evidence only where another path joins their entities, true of 2.5 percent of named-entity facts in our compact WebQSP and CWQ contexts.

摘要：

##### **MAC-Bench: Benchmarking Multi-Agent Collaboration via Interconnected Tasks**
2611.01728v1 by Zerui Chen, Qinggang Zhang, Qichuan Liu, Zhishang Xiang, Zhimin Wei, Yunbo Tang, Zhuo Li, Jinsong Su, Zhihong Zhang

Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) tackle challenging tasks by dividing work among agents and coordinating their contributions toward shared objectives. Evaluating these systems therefore requires benchmarks that assess not only task-solving performance but also the ability to handle interdependent work. However, existing benchmarks often emphasize task difficulty without substantial collaboration requirements or evaluate only a limited range of predefined collaboration patterns. In this paper, we introduce MAC-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating multi-agent collaboration through interconnected tasks. Rather than imposing predefined agent roles or communication protocols, MAC-Bench embeds collaboration requirements directly into task dependencies and shared-resource constraints. Specifically, we mine reusable collaboration structures from selected benchmark tasks at two levels. Local collaboration patterns capture recurring interactions among subtasks, while global task structures characterize how these interactions contribute to complete workflows. Through coarse-to-fine composition, these structures are recombined into task graphs combining different collaboration requirements. Tasks are instantiated in executable environments with automated verification of partial progress and complete task resolution.

#### Abstracts
##### **Personas Are Not People: Identity as State, Not Attribute**
2611.05292v1 by Tamunotonye Harry, Yuanyuan Feng

Synthetic populations, large sets of simulated people, are increasingly used in place of real people to pretest surveys, simulate users and communities, and evaluate AI systems. Each simulated person is defined by a persona, a written profile of fixed traits, demographics, and attitudes that a language model is instructed to act out. Every such system assumes that a person's identity is a stable attribute that behaves the same way in every situation the simulation presents. We show that this assumption is not a harmless simplification. Three decades of personality research find the opposite. How a given person behaves varies more across contexts than people vary from one another. Drawing on identity economics, which describes how different situations activate different identities and norms within the same person, we separate behavior into three parts, which are stable traits, the identity a context calls up, and the situation itself. We then prove, under two stated assumptions, that any simulation built on fixed personas, no matter how detailed the personas or how large the underlying model, cannot reproduce the part of a population's behavior that is specific to it and its circumstances. We propose a new evaluation criterion based on this result and a person-in-context design that can meet it in principle. We test one version on a naturalistic Reddit personality corpus and on a published clinical-trial recruitment survey of a community with documented distrust of medical institutions. The design does produce context-dependent behavior when tested directly, but in neither setting does it fully match the real population, and we show where it falls short. The consequence for practice is a risk that synthetic-population evaluations misrepresent the groups whose behavior is most shaped by context, a distortion that richer personas cannot correct.
